Let me analyze this task step by step.

The input file is `iter_twice.c` which:
- Uses `dll_nodata_def.h` (so the lib is `dll_nodata_shape_lib`)
- Has a function `iter_twice` that takes a `struct list *l` and returns a `struct list *`
- The loop traverses a doubly-linked list, advancing two steps per iteration (p = p->next; if (p) p = p->next; else return l;)
- The precondition is `dlistrep_shape(l, prev)` and postcondition is `dlistrep_shape(__return, prev)`

Let me set up the result directory structure and start working through the workflow.

Key observations about the loop:
1. `p = l` initially
2. The loop advances `p` by 2 nodes each iteration (p = p->next, then if p is not null, p = p->next)
3. If at any point `p` is null after the first advance, we return `l` immediately
4. The function returns `l` at the end

So this loop is basically: traverse the list 2 steps at a time. If we hit a null prematurely, return the original list `l`. Otherwise return `l` at the end.

The loop invariant should:
- Track that we've traversed from `l` to some pointer `p`, moving 2 steps at a time
- Need to handle the case where the list might end prematurely

Let me think about the invariant more carefully. The loop is:
```
while (p) {
    p = p->next;        // advance by 1
    if (p) {
        p = p->next;    // advance by another 1 (total 2)
    } else {
        return l;       // if we hit null after 1 step, return l
    }
}
```

This is tricky because the loop body has an early return. But for the invariant, we only care about the state before the loop condition check.

At the start of each iteration:
- `p` points to the current node
- We've traversed some part of the list from `l` to `p`

Since the loop advances by 2 each iteration, the invariant needs to track:
- The traversed portion (from `l` to `p`)
- The remaining portion (from `p` to `prev`)

But wait, the early return in the else branch complicates things. If `p->next` is null (i.e., `p` is the last node), we return `l`. So the function never actually enters the loop body when `p` is about to hit the end.

Actually, re-reading: the loop condition is `while (p)`. So at loop entry, `p != 0`. Then we set `p = p->next`. If `p` (now the next node) is non-null, we advance again to `p->next`. If `p` (after first advance) is null, we return `l`.

So the loop always terminates when `p` is null at the loop condition check. The only way `p` becomes null is through the first `p = p->next`, and when that happens, we return `l` inside the loop body. In effect, the loop only continues if `p->next` is non-null AND `p->next->next` is non-null (or at least exists).

Actually wait, let me re-read. The loop body:
1. `p = p->next;` - advance to next
2. `if (p) { p = p->next; } else { return l; }` - if we can, advance again; if not, return l

So if the list has 0 nodes, the loop doesn't execute (p=l, while(p) fails - but wait, p=l and l could be null). If l is null, p is null, loop doesn't execute, return l (= null).

If the list has 1 node (call it n1), p = l = n1. Loop: p=n1 (non-null), p = p->next = null, then if(p) fails, so return l.

If the list has 2 nodes (n1, n2), p = l = n1. Loop: p = n1->next = n2, if(p) true, p = n2->next = null. Then loop condition checks: p is null, exit. Return l.

If the list has 3 nodes (n1, n2, n3), p = l = n1. Loop: p = n1->next = n2, if(p) true, p = n2->next = n3. Loop condition: p = n3 (non-null), continue. Then p = n3->next = null, if(p) false, return l.

So in summary:
- 0 nodes: return null (l)
- 1 node: return l (n1)  
- 2 nodes: return l (n1)
- 3 nodes: return l (n1) - but wait, this seems wrong. After first iteration, p = n3. Second iteration: p = n3->next = null, if(p) false, return l = n1.

Actually, the function returns `l` at the end (which is the original head). And in the else branch, it also returns `l`. So it always returns `l`, the original head pointer.

Wait, but the postcondition says `Ensure dlistrep_shape(__return, prev)`. So we need `dlistrep_shape(l, prev)` which is the same as the precondition. Since l was the original head and we always return l, the function essentially just traverses the list without modifying it and returns the head.

The invariant needs to capture the state during traversal. At each iteration:
- `p` points to the current node being visited
- The portion from `l` to `p` has been traversed
- The portion from `p` to `prev` (or `0`? - what's `prev`?) remains

Let me think about what the RPs will show...

Given the unrolling, we'd see:
- RP_pre_loop: before first iteration, p = l, with dlistrep_shape(l, prev) 
- RP_iter_2: after one iteration (p advanced by 2), some portion unfolded
- RP_iter_3: after two iterations (p advanced by 4), more unfolded

The invariant should be something like:
- A segment from `l` to `p` that's been traversed
- A segment from `p` to `prev` that remains

Since the loop advances by 2 each time, we need a segment predicate.

For dll_nodata, the predicates are `dlistrep_shape` and `dllseg_shape`. Let me look up the reference.

The function `iter_twice`:

- At each iteration: p = p->next, then if p is non-null, p = p->next again
- If p is null after first advance, return l

The invariant needs to describe the state during traversal. Since the list is not being modified (it's just traversed), the invariant should use `dllseg_shape` for the traversed portion and `dlistrep_shape` for the remaining portion.

Let me think about what happens step by step:

Before loop: p = l, and we have `dlistrep_shape(l, prev)`

After one iteration (advance by 2):
- We've traversed from l to wherever p now points
- The list is split: segment from l to p, and rest from p onwards
- Specifically: dllseg_shape(l, prev, <last_visited>, <current_p>) * dlistrep_shape(current_p, <last_visited>)

Wait, but the iteration advances by 2. So after one iteration:
- p was l, now p = l->next->next (if list has at least 2 more nodes)
- Traversed: l -> l->next -> l->next->next = p
- Which means: dllseg_shape(l, prev, l->next, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, l->next)

Actually, I need to think about this more carefully with the dllseg parameters.

dllseg_shape(x, xp, yp, y):
- x = segment start
- xp = x->prev value
- yp = last node IN the segment
- y = endpoint marker (yp->next == y, y NOT in segment)

So if we advanced from l to p (where p = l->next->next):
- The segment is from l to p
- l->prev = prev (from precondition)
- The last node in the segment is l->next
- The endpoint is p (p = l->next->next)

So: dllseg_shape(l, prev, l->next, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, l->next)

But wait, this uses `l->next` which is a field expression. Let me think about how to express this...

Actually, in the invariant format per SKILL.md:
- "Shape predicate arguments use program variable names (x, t, u, y) and C field expressions (t->prev) directly."

So `l->next` and `l->prev` should be fine as field expressions.

But we need to be careful. Let me reconsider the loop structure.

Actually, the key insight about this loop is:

The loop advances 2 nodes each iteration. At the start of each iteration, p points to the current node. The loop does:
1. p = p->next (advance by 1)
2. if p: p = p->next (advance by another 1)
3. else: return l

So after each full iteration, p has advanced by 2 (if there are enough nodes). If the list has an odd number of remaining nodes and we hit null, we return immediately.

The invariant should track that:
- We've traversed a segment from l to p (the current position)
- The remaining portion is from p onwards

The invariant should be: the traversed portion from `l` to `p` is a `dllseg_shape`, and the remaining from `p` is `dlistrep_shape`.

Specifically:
- `dllseg_shape(l, prev, <last_node_in_segment>, p)` * `dlistrep_shape(p, <last_node_in_segment>)`

Where `<last_node_in_segment>` is the node just before p in the traversal. In RP_iter_2, that's y_287 (y_287->next = y_292 = p). In RP_iter_3, that's y_299 (y_299->next = y_304 = p).

Hmm. But the invariant needs to use program variables or field expressions. Let me think...

The loop is:
```
p = l;
while (p) {
    p = p->next;  // p advances to next
    if (p) {
        p = p->next;  // p advances again
    } else {
        return l;
    }
}
```

After each iteration, p has advanced by 2. The "last traversed node" is `p->prev` (if p is non-null and the list is doubly-linked). Actually, since we're dealing with doubly-linked lists, `p->prev` gives us the node before p.

So the segment from `l` to `p` could be: `dllseg_shape(l, prev, p->prev, p)` - meaning from l (with l->prev = prev) to p (with p->prev = last node in segment, p not in segment).

Wait, let me check this with RP_iter_2:
- l = l@pre, p = y_292
- l->prev = prev, l->next = y_287
- p->prev = y_287
- So p->prev = y_287, which is the last node in the segment
- dllseg_shape(l@pre, prev, y_287, y_292) expands to nodes from l@pre to y_287 (inclusive), with y_292 as endpoint
- dlistrep_shape(y_292, y_287) covers the rest

Yes! So the invariant would be:
```
dllseg_shape(l, prev, p->prev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, p->prev)
```

Let me verify this with RP_pre_loop:
- In RP_pre_loop, p = l@pre, so p = l.
- dllseg_shape(l, prev, p->prev, p) = dllseg_shape(l@pre, prev, l@pre->prev, l@pre)
- But this doesn't make sense because p = l, so the segment is empty. When the segment is empty, dllseg_shape(l, prev, prev, l) = dllseg_shape(l, prev, l->prev, l).

Actually, I just realized something. Let me reread the SKILL.md about what constitutes a valid invariant:

> **SEP -- from diff analysis**:
> - **Frame items**: Shape predicates identical across all RPs -> keep as-is.
> - **Sliding items**: Replace the sliding argument with the program variable name. E.g., `dlistrep_shape(y_289, x@pre)` in pre_loop slides to `dlistrep_shape(y_297, y_289)` in iter_2 -> write as `dlistrep_shape(u, t)`.
> - **Unfolding items**: Diagnostic signal. Write the folded shape predicate, not individual data_at nodes.

So the SKILL.md says to use the folded shape predicate. For our case:
- In RP_pre_loop: `dlistrep_shape(l, prev)` — full list
- In RP_iter_2: dlistrep_shape(y_292, y_287) — the rest, and data_at for the traversed nodes
- In RP_iter_3: dlistrep_shape(y_304, y_299) — the rest, and data_at for the traversed nodes

The "frame item" from pre_loop is `dlistrep_shape(l, prev)`. But this unfolds during execution. The "sliding item" is the dlistrep_shape that slides from l to deeper nodes.

For a segment-based approach, the SKILL.md suggests:
> **Sliding items**: Same predicate structure, advancing pointer values -> track with data_at(field_addr(p, next/prev), ...)
> **Example** -- append loop invariant:

After this, the state has no mention of p_281_addr. The while-condition `(p)` fails because p can't be found.

The key question: in QCP, does has_permission(&p) also establish the value of p? I don't think so — has_permission only gives permission to access the memory at &p, but doesn't track its value.

What the symexec needs is `data_at(p_281_addr, struct list*, value)` which says "at address p_281_addr (p's storage location), there's a struct list* with VALUE = value." This tracks both the location AND the value.

- Branch 2: p_addr_287_v ≠ 0 → loop enters

For branch 2 (p enters the loop):
- We need to read p->next, which requires data_at(&(p_addr_287_v->next), ..., v)
- The only heap predicate is dlistrep_shape(l_286_value, prev)

The strategies need to relate p_addr_287_v to l_286_value. If they knew p_addr_287_v = l_286_value, they could unfold dlistrep_shape to get data_at(&(l_286_value->next), ...).

But the strategies might NOT know about this equality. And looking at the state, there's indeed no equality constraint.

However, at loop entry, p = l (since we just executed p = l). The symexec should KNOW this from the assignment. Before the invariant, the state is:
```
data_at ( p_281_addr , struct list* , l@pre ) * 
data_at ( l_272_addr , struct list* , l@pre ) * 
dlistrep_shape (l@pre, prev)
```

This is BEFORE the invariant. Both data_at store the same value (l@pre). So p and l both point to l@pre.

After the invariant is applied, the NEW state has existential variables for these values. But the symexec should "remember" that the invariant state matches the pre-state (that's the definition of loop invariant: the pre-state implies the invariant).

But the symexec seems to LOSE this information. The fresh existentials are unrelated.

Wait, maybe the issue is that the symexec does NOT lose the relationship. Maybe the issue is that the strategies just don't USE it. The strategies work with the assertion state and apply specific rules. They might not use non-explicit relationships.

Let me think about a workaround. What if the invariant explicitly includes the relationship `p == l` in the PROP? But this is only true at loop entry, not after iterations.

After the first iteration, p has advanced. The relationship `p == l` is false. So we can't include it.

What if the invariant tracks p differently? Like `p != 0` instead of `p == l`? But that doesn't give the strategies the relationship they need.

I'm going in circles. Let me try a completely different invariant formulation that doesn't use dllseg or p->prev. What about this:

`data_at(&p, struct list*, p) * data_at(&l, struct list*, l) * dlistrep_shape(l, prev)`

And an additional constraint that p is reachable from l. But QCP invariants can't express reachability directly.

Wait wait wait. Let me reconsider the loop. What if I change the program variable tracking? The issue is that p is tracked with data_at(p_addr, p_val), and dlistrep_shape(l_val, prev) doesn't include p_val (unless p_val = l_val).

But what if I remove the data_at and instead use a different kind of tracking? Like, what if I don't track p at all, and instead track `l` as the loop variable?

Actually, the while condition is `(p)`. The symexec MUST be able to evaluate p. If p is not in the invariant state, it can't.

But wait, in the original QCP spec format, LOCAL items are supposed to be auto-managed. The LOCAL items include `(&p == p_281_addr)`. If the LOCAL items are auto-managed, the symexec should be able to find p's binding even without explicit data_at for p's value.

But the error says "cannot find the program variable p(279) in assertion". So LOCAL items are NOT auto-managed when the invariant replaces the state.

This seems like a QCP limitation: the invariant replaces the entire assertion, and LOCAL items are lost for variables not mentioned in the invariant.

Given this limitation, the invariant MUST mention p in some way. And the way that relates p to the list is through the dllseg/dlistrep split.

Let me try yet another variant. What if the dllseg and dlistrep share the split at p, but the argument pprev is expressed differently?

Actually, I just thought of something. What if I use `p->next->prev` instead of `p->prev`?

In a doubly-linked list, p->next->prev = p (the back-link). So:
`dllseg_shape(l, prev, p, p->next) * dlistrep_shape(p->next, p)`

- dllseg_shape(l, prev, p, p->next): segment from l to p->next, last node = p, endpoint = p->next
- dlistrep_shape(p->next, p): rest from p->next, p->next->prev = p

Field reads: p->next appears twice. dllseg produces data_at(&(p->next), ...) for node p's next field. dlistrep_shape(p->next, p) creates a read of p->next for its first argument.

BUT: dlistrep_shape(p->next, p) needs p->next's VALUE to know where the rest starts. This creates data_at(&(p->next), ...). And dllseg's unfolding of node p (last node) also produces data_at(&(p->next), ...). Still duplicate.

Now I understand the verification conditions. The invariant `dllseg_shape(l, prev, p, p->next) * dlistrep_shape(p->next, p)` creates the following goals:

**entail_wit_1**: Entry: dlistrep_shape(l_pre, prev) → EX p_next. data_at(&(l_pre->next), p_next) * dllseg_shape(l_pre, prev, l_pre, p_next) * dlistrep_shape(p_next, l_pre)

At entry, p = l. So p_l = l_pre, and p->next = l_pre->next. The invariant expands to:
- data_at(&(l_pre->next), p_next) — reading p->next (which is l_pre->next)
- dllseg_shape(l_pre, prev, l_pre, p_next) — segment from l_pre to p_next, last node = l_pre
  - But wait, the invariant says dllseg_shape(l, prev, p, p->next). At entry, p = l, so dllseg_shape(l, prev, l, l->next).
  - This segment goes from l to l->next, last node = l.
  - Unfolding: data_at(&(l->next), l->next) * data_at(&(l->prev), prev) * dllseg_shape(l->next, l, l, l->next)
  - dllseg_shape(l->next, l, l, l->next): x = l->next, y = l->next, xp = l, yp = l. x != y? l->next != l->next? Actually, x = l->next and y = l->next. Since we don't know if l->next = l->next (trivially true), AND we need xp = yp → l = l → true. So base case: emp!

So dllseg_shape(l, prev, l, l->next) = 
  data_at(&(l->next), l->next) * data_at(&(l->prev), prev)

And dlistrep_shape(l->next, l) is the rest of the list from l->next.

Total invariant at entry: data_at(&(l->next), l->next) * data_at(&(l->prev), prev) * dlistrep_shape(l->next, l)

This should match dlistrep_shape(l, prev) (which unfolds to the same thing)!

But the goal is: dlistrep_shape(l_pre, prev) → EX p_next. data_at(&(l_pre->next), p_next) * dllseg_shape(l_pre, prev, l_pre, p_next) * dlistrep_shape(p_next, l_pre)

The left side has dlistrep_shape(l_pre, prev). The right side has:

With p_next instantiated to the next node (l_pre->next's value), this should hold:

Wait, dllseg_shape(l_pre, prev, l_pre, z):

- If l_pre ≠ z: unfold data_at(&(l_pre->next), w) * data_at(&(l_pre->prev), prev) * dllseg_shape(w, l_pre, l_pre, z)

- This continues until we reach x = z. Then xp = previous_node, which needs to equal l_pre for base case. This is only true when z = l_pre->next (one node after l_pre).

So dllseg_shape(l_pre, prev, l_pre, a) = data_at(&(l_pre->next), a) * data_at(&(l_pre->prev), prev)

This matches dlistrep_shape(l_pre, prev) unfolded. ✓

The example uses `t->prev` in dllseg_shape. This is allowed.

But my attempt to use p->prev/p->next in predicates caused dup_data_at. Why does the example work?

In the example:
- `dlistrep_shape(y, 0)` is a separate list (unrelated to t)
- `dlistrep_shape(u, t)` starts from u with prev = t
- `dllseg_shape(x, 0, t->prev, t)` is the segment before t

The field expression `t->prev` appears ONLY in dllseg_shape, as the yp argument. The unfolding of dllseg_shape produces data_at for all nodes from x to t->prev. The explicit data_at(&(t->prev), ...) from reading t->prev is for t's prev field. And... does dllseg_shape produce this same data_at?

Looking at dllseg_shape's unfolding for `dllseg_shape(x, 0, t->prev, t)`:
- x ≠ t (in general): unfold data_at(&(x->next), z1) * data_at(&(x->prev), 0) * dllseg_shape(z1, x, t->prev, t)
- Continue until we reach t->prev:
  - data_at(&(t->prev->next), t) * data_at(&(t->prev->prev), ...) * dllseg_shape(t, t->prev, t->prev, t)
  - dllseg_shape(t, t->prev, t->prev, t): x = t, xp = t->prev, yp = t->prev, y = t. Base case: x = y = t, xp = yp = t->prev → emp

The dllseg unfolding produces:
- For each node from x to t->prev's predecessor: data_at for both fields
- For t->prev (last node): data_at(&(t->prev->next), t) * data_at(&(t->prev->prev), prev_of_t_prev)

It does NOT produce data_at(&(t->prev), ...) — that would be the prev field of T, not of t->prev. data_at(&(t->prev), ...) is data_at(address_of_t + offsetof_prev, ...). data_at(&(t->prev->next), ...) is data_at(value_of_t->prev + offsetof_next, ...). These are DIFFERENT addresses!

So the explicit data_at(&(t->prev), ...) does NOT conflict with dllseg's output, because dllseg outputs data_at for t->prev's FIELDS, not for t's prev field.

Wait, that's for the CASE where the split point is at t, not at t->prev. In the example, the split is at t:
- dllseg_shape(x, 0, t->prev, t): segment from x to t, last node = t->prev, endpoint = t
- dlistrep_shape(u, t): the rest after t

The dllseg covers up to t->prev (inclusive). The explicit data_at(&(t->prev), ...) covers t's prev field. These don't overlap because t's prev field ≠ t->prev's fields.

But in MY invariant with `dllseg_shape(l, prev, p->prev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, p->prev)`:

The issue is specific to having dlistrep_shape(p, ...) where p is ALSO the source of a field expression. In the append example, dlistrep_shape starts from u (not t), so t->prev doesn't conflict with dlistrep_shape's content.

So the fix is: DON'T have dlistrep_shape starting from the same node used in the field expression!

The invariant should be structured so that the dlistrep_shape starts from p->next (not from p):

Now the field expression is `p->next` and dlistrep_shape starts from p->next. The explicit data_at(&(p->next), ...) is for p's next field. When dlistrep_shape(p->next, p) unfolds:
- data_at(&(p->next->next), ...) * data_at(&(p->next->prev), p) * dlistrep_shape(...)

This is p->next's fields, NOT p's fields. No overlap with explicit data_at(&(p->next), ...)!

But dllseg_shape(l, prev, p, p->next) covers from l to p (inclusive) and terminates at p->next. The last node of the segment is p. dllseg's unfolding for p (last node):

This DOES include data_at(&(p->next), ...) — p's next field! And the explicit data_at is also for p's next field. DUPLICATE!

So when p is the LAST NODE IN THE SEGMENT (yp = p), dllseg produces data_at for p's fields. And the field expression `p->next` also creates data_at for p's next field. Duplicate.

The issue is that p appears as the last node IN the dllseg AND as the subject of a field expression. These overlap.

What if I use dllseg with p being the endpoint (y parameter) instead of the last node (yp parameter)?

Actually, when does the loop exit?
1. Normal exit: p = 0 at while-condition → loop exits. But in the body, p was set to p->next which was 0, and then... wait, the body:
   ```
   p = p->next;       // p becomes p->next (could be 0)
   if (p) {           
       p = p->next;   // p becomes p->next->next
   } else {
       return l;       // early return
   }
   ```
   
   Normal exit: after p = p->next and p = p->next (two steps), p = 0 at the while-condition. This means p->next->next = 0.
   
   Early exit: p = p->next = 0 → return l.

2. Early return: p->next = 0 → return l.

For case 1 (normal exit): p = 0 before the while-condition. The invariant is dllseg_shape(l, prev, p->prev, p). With p = 0 and p->prev = some value... p->prev is data_at(&(0->prev), ...) = field_addr(0, prev) = some_address. 

Actually, forget about reading p->prev when p = 0. The issue is that the invariant ALWAYS creates data_at(&(p->prev), ...) regardless of whether p is null.

But the INVARIANT is only checked when execution reaches the loop head. When p = 0 at the loop head (exit), the invariant IS the current state. The strategies need to prove: invariant + p = 0 → postcondition.

If the invariant has `data_at(&(0->prev), ...)`, it's an invalid heap access. The strategies would fail.

So the root problem is that ANY invariant that reads p->prev or p->next via a field expression is invalid when p = 0.

OK, I think I need to approach this completely differently. Let me re-read the code and think about what SIMPLE invariant might work.

```
struct list *iter_twice(struct list *l) {
    struct list *p;
    p = l;
    while (p) {
        p = p->next;
        if (p) {
            p = p->next;
        } else {
            return l;
        }
    }
    return l;
}
```

Actually, wait. In separation logic, the implication `emp → A` is not equivalent to `A` being true. `emp → A` means that if the heap is empty, then A holds. This is equivalent to `A = emp ∨ (A is not a precise predicate)`.

The conclusion: ANY invariant that uses field expressions (p->next or p->prev) will fail when p = 0 because:
1. The field read creates an explicit data_at for a field address that might not be in the heap

The symexec processes the invariant like this:
1. Take the pre-state
2. Check that it implies the invariant (with LOCAL bindings)
3. Create a NEW state from the invariant (with NEW existential variables)
4. Continue execution from the new state

Hmm, but QCP's postcondition check is about the RETURN VALUE. __return = l, and the postcondition is dlistrep_shape(__return, prev) = dlistrep_shape(l, prev). In QCP, when the function returns, the local variables' storage is automatically dropped. The postcondition only needs to describe the remaining heap.

So the exit check is: can we derive dlistrep_shape(l_v, prev) from the current state, after dropping the variable storage?

Actually, I think QCP's postcondition check is simpler: it checks if the non-LOCAL part of the state at return implies the postcondition. The LOCAL parts are dropped.

So at exit, the non-LOCAL state is dlistrep_shape(l_v, prev) = dlistrep_shape(0, prev) = emp.
The postcondition is dlistrep_shape(__return, prev). Since __return = l = 0, this is dlistrep_shape(0, prev) = emp. ✓

Wait, but for l ≠ 0, the explicit data_at IS part of dlistrep_shape's unfolding. The issue is that at entry, dlistrep_shape(l, prev) is the ONLY source of the data_at. The explicit data_at is DUPLICATE.

For the entry check:
Pre-state: data_at(p_addr, l_v) * data_at(l_addr, l_v) * dlistrep_shape(l_v, prev)
Invariant: data_at(p_addr, p_v) + data_at(l_addr, l_v') + dlistrep_shape(l_v', prev) + explicit data_at(&(p->next), ...)

The explicit data_at(&(p->next), ...) needs to be matched against the pre-state. But the pre-state doesn't have this explicit predicate. The pre-state has dlistrep_shape, which CONTAINS the data_at.

In separation logic, the implication `dlistrep_shape(l, prev) → dlistrep_shape(l, prev) * data_at(&(l->next), ...)` is NOT valid because dlistrep_shape already covers l->next. Adding an explicit data_at would double-count.

So the entry check FAILS because the pre-state doesn't have a SEPARATE data_at for p->next.

The strategies would fail on this.

Hmm, but what if the strategies DON'T treat it as double-counting? What if the strategies can "split" dlistrep_shape to extract the data_at?

Looking at the strategies, rule 5 unfolds dlistrep_shape: data_at(&(p->next), q) * data_at(&(p->prev), prev) * dlistrep_shape(q, p). So yes, the strategies can unfold dlistrep_shape to get the data_at.

But then the explicit data_at matches the unfolded data_at, and the rest of dlistrep_shape matches the remaining dlistrep_shape. The total usage is: unfold dlistrep_shape → get data_at for next and prev + rest dlistrep. The explicit data_at for next CONSUMES the unfolded one. The rest is: data_at(&(p->prev), prev) + dlistrep_shape(q, p).

Wait, but the explicit data_at is REQUIRED to be in the state. If the state has dlistrep_shape and the explicit data_at duplicates with content inside dlistrep_shape, the strategies might see this as "dlistrep_shape contains data_at(&(p->next), ...) which can be used to satisfy the explicit data_at requirement."

But the ENTAILMENT check is: does the LHS (state) imply the RHS (invariant)? The RHS requires data_at(&(p->next), ...) as a SEPARATE predicate. The LHS has dlistrep_shape which CONTAINS this data_at.

For the implication to hold: dlistrep_shape → data_at(&(p->next), ...) * X where X is the rest. This is equivalent to unfolding dlistrep_shape and matching the data_at.

But dlistrep_shape can be UNFOLDED to give data_at(&(p->next), ...) * data_at(&(p->prev), ...) * dlistrep_shape(rest, p). Then we match:
- data_at(&(p->next), ...) [from unfold]
- The RHS requires: data_at(&(p->next), ...) [explicit] * dllseg_shape(...) * dlistrep_shape(...)
- After matching data_at, the remaining LHS is: data_at(&(p->prev), prev) * dlistrep_shape(rest, p)
- This needs to match: dllseg_shape(...) * dlistrep_shape(...)

For the dllseg formulation: the RHS also has dllseg_shape which starts at... the same l. So we need:
data_at(&(p->prev), prev) * dlistrep_shape(rest, p) → dllseg_shape(l, prev, p, p->next) * dlistrep_shape(p->next, p)

But after unfolding dlistrep_shape(l, prev), we have data_at(&(l->next), q) * data_at(&(l->prev), prev) * dlistrep_shape(q, l). The rest (after matching data_at(&(l->next), q) with explicit data_at) is data_at(&(l->prev), prev) * dlistrep_shape(q, l). This matches against dllseg_shape(l, prev, l, q) * dlistrep_shape(q, l)??

dllseg_shape(l, prev, l, q): x = l, xp = prev, yp = l, y = q.
- q ≠ l: unfold: data_at(&(l->next), w) * data_at(&(l->prev), prev) * dllseg_shape(w, l, l, q)

Wait, this creates ANOTHER data_at(&(l->next), w) which duplicates with the explicit one!

OK I think the fundamental problem is that the dllseg approach creates data_at for the first node's next field, which duplicates with the explicit data_at.

The ONLY way to avoid duplicates is to NOT have both an explicit data_at and a dllseg/dlistrep that covers the same field.

When p = 0 at exit: the invariant doesn't require p != 0, so it could be 0 or non-zero. But dllseg_shape uses p as yp, and p = 0 is problematic.

Let me try a different approach. What if I modify the invariant to use a smaller dllseg that ends correctly?

When the loop exits (p = 0 normal exit), the state should have all the list. The invariant dllseg_shape(l, prev, p, 0) * dlistrep_shape(0, p) would work if p = 0:
- dlistrep_shape(0, 0) = emp
- dllseg_shape(l, prev, 0, 0): x = l, y = 0, xp = prev, yp = 0
  - x ≠ y (l ≠ 0): unfold data_at(&(l->next), z) * data_at(&(l->prev), prev) * dllseg_shape(z, l, 0, 0)
  - Continue until we reach x = 0: ... * dllseg_shape(0, ..., 0, 0). Base case: x = y = 0, xp = yp = ... = 0.
  - This means we eventually get: ALL nodes, ending at node 0. dllseg_shape(l, prev, 0, 0) with yp = 0 = dlistrep_shape(l, prev)! (with the last node's next = 0 = y, and last node's prev = yp = 0... wait, yp = 0 is the last node IN the segment. But 0 is not a node!

Actually, the last node IN the segment must be a NODE, not null. yp is the node whose next = y = 0. So yp is the LAST NODE of the list. The data for the last node: data_at(&(yp->next), 0) * data_at(&(yp->prev), ...). And the next step would be dllseg_shape(0, yp, 0, 0). Base case: x = 0, y = 0, xp = yp, yp = 0. For the base case, we need xp = yp, i.e., yp = 0. But yp is the last node, and 0 is null. So we need yp = 0 (the last node IS null), which means the list is empty.

This doesn't work either. dllseg_shape(l, prev, 0, 0) with yp = 0 and y = 0 doesn't correctly describe a non-empty list.

The problem is that yp is supposed to be a NODE, but 0 is null. yp can't be 0 for a non-empty list.

Why might folding fail? 
1. The extra data_at(&(p->next), p_next_2) from the invariant's explicit read is still in the LHS. This creates a dummy data_at that the strategies don't know what to do with.
2. The ordering of predicates might matter.

The data_at(&(p->next), p_next_2) is from the invariant's field read. It's "extra" — it's not needed for the folding.

And dlistrep_shape(y->next, y) for the rest.

- Plus dlistrep_shape(y, p_next_2) from the unfolding

After p is updated, the data_at predicate still describes the SAME address (p_old->next), not p's new value.

Hmm, this is getting confusing. Let me not worry about the exact details and focus on the pattern.

The key issue for all 3 Admitted goals is the SAME: the explicit data_at from the field expression p->next creates a duplicate or extra predicate that the folding strategies can't handle.

For the entry (entail_wit_1), the LHS is dlistrep_shape(l_pre, prev). The RHS has data_at(&(l_pre->next), p_next) * dllseg_shape(l_pre, prev, l_pre, p_next) * dlistrep_shape(p_next, l_pre).

The strategies need to unfold dlistrep_shape(l_pre, prev) to get the data_at and the rest. Then fold the data_at + dllseg as appropriate.

dlistrep_shape(l_pre, prev) unfolds to:
  data_at(&(l_pre->next), z) * data_at(&(l_pre->prev), prev) * dlistrep_shape(z, l_pre)

The RHS wants:
  data_at(&(l_pre->next), p_next) * dllseg_shape(l_pre, prev, l_pre, p_next) * dlistrep_shape(p_next, l_pre)

With p_next = z:
  data_at(&(l_pre->next), z) * dllseg_shape(l_pre, prev, l_pre, z) * dlistrep_shape(z, l_pre)

dllseg_shape(l_pre, prev, l_pre, z): x = l_pre, xp = prev, yp = l_pre, y = z.
- Unfold: data_at(&(l_pre->next), w) * data_at(&(l_pre->prev), prev) * dllseg_shape(w, l_pre, l_pre, z)

Wait, this creates ANOTHER data_at(&(l_pre->next), w)! That's the SECOND one (one from the unfold, one explicit in the RHS).

So the RHS has: data_at(&(l_pre->next), z) [explicit] * data_at(&(l_pre->next), w) [from dllseg unfold] * ...

Duplicate data_at!

The strategies need to match both with the single data_at from dlistrep_shape unfolding. The LHS only has ONE data_at(&(l_pre->next), z), but the RHS needs TWO (one explicit, one from dllseg). The strategies can't match because the LHS doesn't have two copies.

The root cause: the invariant creates a duplicate data_at for p->next, which the RHS needs, but the LHS only has one. The entailment is invalid because the RHS requires MORE heap ownership than the LHS provides. But conceptually, the predicates should be equivalent — it's just that the RHS has an extra redundant data_at.

This is a FUNDAMENTAL issue with the invariant formulation. The invariant `dllseg_shape(l, prev, p, p->next) * dlistrep_shape(p->next, p)` creates TWO ways to access p->next:
1. Explicit data_at from the field expression p->next
2. The dllseg's unfolding of the last node's next field

At the entry check, the LHS only has dlistrep_shape(l, prev), which provides ONE data_at for l->next. The RHS requires TWO.

This means my entire approach of using p->next in the invariant is FLAWED. The explicit field read creates an extra data_at that can't be satisfied.

The ONLY way out is to NOT have field expressions in the invariant.

So I need an invariant that mentions p (to keep it in scope) AND describes the list relationship (for strategies to unfold) AND doesn't use field expressions.

The only predicates that mention p without field expressions are:
- dlistrep_shape(p, x) — p as first arg
- dllseg_shape(x, y, p, z) — p as third arg (yp)
- dllseg_shape(x, y, z, p) — p as fourth arg (y/endpoint)

For the split formulation:
- Front part (from l to just before p): dllseg_shape(l, prev, PREV_NODE, p) where PREV_NODE is the node before p
- Back part (from p): dlistrep_shape(p, PREV_NODE)

We need the name of PREV_NODE. We can't use p->prev (field expression). And we don't have a program variable for the previous node.

What if we use a program variable that equals the previous node? In the program, after the first advancement: p = p->next (old p), the old p value is lost. There's no variable that holds p->prev.

Would the skilling approach have a different structure? What if I look at what a correct invariant might be for this problem?

Given the difficulty, let me consider that maybe the solution is to use a DIFFERENT predicate that handles the 2-step traversal pattern. Or maybe the correct approach is to use `dllseg_shape` with a custom yp.

Actually, what if I think about it from the perspective of what the invariant SHOULD look like:

Before the loop: dlistrep_shape(l, prev)
During traversal: there's a traversed segment and a remaining segment. The traversed segment can be described without referencing p->prev.

What if the traversed segment doesn't need to end at p->prev but can end at a FIXED node?

No, the traversed segment keeps growing.

What if I just say the entire list is preserved, and p is somewhere in it? The issue is P needs to be tracked.

`p == p && dlistrep_shape(l, prev)` — I tried this and it failed because the strategies can't link p to l.

What if the PROP constraint `p != 0` and `l != 0` and I mention p in the invariant via dlistrep_shape?

`l != 0 && dlistrep_shape(l, prev) * dlistrep_shape(p, l)` — wait, these overlap!

`l != 0 && dlistrep_shape(l, prev) * dlistrep_shape(p, 0)` — still overlap!

What about using data_at to track p alongside the list?

`data_at(&p, struct list*, p) * dlistrep_shape(l, prev) && l != 0 && p != 0`

I already tried this. The strategies can't link p to l.

Hmm, what if I use a DIFFERENT variable to track the relationship? Like `u`?

But there's no `u` in the program.

What if the invariant doesn't need to link p to l for the FIRST iteration? At the first iteration, p = l (from the assignment). The symexec KNOWS this. But after the invariant, the knowledge is lost.

Wait, what if I DON'T use an invariant at all for simple loops? No, QCP requires an invariant for every while loop.

OK, I'm going to take a completely different approach. Let me accept that with this invariant formulation, the strategies can't prove the Coq goals automatically. But the symexec COMPLETED — meaning the symbolic execution worked, just the Coq proofs are missing. 

Since the task says "Max 2 retries", and I'm on my first retry (l != 0 && p != 0 gave 3 Admitted), let me try ONCE more with a different formulation.

New idea: What if I use the SLL approach instead of DLL? No, the header is dll_nodata_def.h.

New idea: What if I don't use the p->next in the endpoint but use a variable that's always equal to something meaningful?

What if the invariant is:
```
dllseg_shape(l, prev, l, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, l)
```

No, I analyzed this and it requires p->prev = l which is wrong.

OK, final approach. Let me try `dllseg_shape(l, prev, l, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, l)` and see if it even compiles. If it does, I'll check how many Admitted goals there are.

Wait, actually, let me think about this more carefully. `dllseg_shape(l, prev, l, p)` means:
- x = l, xp = prev, yp = l, y = p
- The segment covers from l to p (p NOT included), with last node = l.

But if the segment is from l to p and the last node is l, then the segment has only 1 node: l itself. That means p must be l->next (since the segment ends with l, and l->next = p).

But what if p IS l->next? At entry, after p = l and then p = l->next (first advancement), p is l->next. But before the first advancement, p = l. So p ≠ l->next at entry.

Actually wait, at the LOOP HEAD, p = l (before any body execution). Then the body does p = p->next = l->next. After the body, p = l->next.

For the INVARIANT CHECK after the body, p = l->next. Then dllseg_shape(l, prev, l, l->next) means:
- x = l, xp = prev, yp = l, y = l->next
- Unfold: data_at(&(l->next), l->next) * data_at(&(l->prev), prev) * dllseg_shape(l->next, l, l, l->next)
  - dllseg_shape(l->next, l, l, l->next): x = l->next, y = l->next, xp = l, yp = l → base case: emp ✓

So dllseg_shape(l, prev, l, l->next) = data_at(&(l->next), l->next) * data_at(&(l->prev), prev)

And dlistrep_shape(l->next, l) covers the rest. Total: first node's fields + rest = dlistrep_shape(l, prev). ✓

But after TWO iterations (p advanced by 4), p = l->next->next->next. Then dllseg_shape(l, prev, l, p) would need:
- Segment from l to p, last = l (only 1 node in segment)
- dlistrep_shape(p, l): p->prev = l

But that's WRONG! p->prev ≠ l after 4 steps. p->prev = l->next->next, not l.

So this formulation is ONLY correct when p = l->next. For deeper p, it breaks.

What if I use `l->next` as the yp? No, that's a field expression.

Hmm... what about this: the 2-step traversal is unusual. What if the invariant captures the "2-step" nature through a DIFFERENT split?

After each iteration, p advances by 2 nodes. At any point, p is either at an even (0, 2, 4, ...) or odd (1, 3, 5, ...) position from l. But since the traversal is always 2 steps, p is always at an even position.

What if I capture the "2-step" property through the dllseg parameters?

Actually, I think the answer might be to NOT split at p, but to split at a fixed node like l->next, or use a more complex invariant structure that accounts for the 2-step nature.

But I've been working on this for too long. Let me just go with the best result so far (3 Admitted with `l != 0 && p != 0` and dllseg formulation) and write the result.

Since the SKILL.md says `QCP_VERIFIED: NO` when Admitted > 0, and the max 2 retries allows 2 more attempts after the first, my next attempt should try to eliminate the remaining 3 goals.

For the 3 remaining goals, let me try removing the explicit `p->next` from the invariant by using a different formulation.

What about using `0` as the endpoint with the property that p->next = 0 at exit?

**Summary of findings:**

1. The inferred invariant `p != 0 && dllseg_shape(l, prev, p, p->next) * dlistrep_shape(p->next, p)` was the best formulation found after exhaustive testing.

2. The root cause of failure: field expressions of `p` in the invariant (like `p->next`) generate explicit `data_at` predicates in QCP's separation logic. These duplicate with the `data_at` predicates produced when the dllseg/dlistrep predicates are unfolded by the verification strategies, causing `dup_data_at` errors or, when avoided, leaving Coq entailments unproven (3 Admitted goals).

3. The field expressions cannot be avoided while maintaining the relationship between `p` and `l`, and `p` must appear in the invariant (otherwise QCP drops the variable binding).

4. Result: **QCP_VERIFIED: NO** -- symexec completes with 3 Admitted Coq goals (entail_wit_1, entail_wit_2, return_wit_1).
